F degree up to max C_r - min_p? We can shift to reduce length. Let p indices in [l,mid], q=C_j in [C_l?]. We can shift p by l-1 and q by C_{mid+1}? But q-p = (q - C0) - (p - p0). If we set P_shift[x]=H[l+x] at x, and F_shift[k]=fact[C0 + k - (l-1)]? The convolution coefficient for q is sum H[p] fact[q-p]. Let p=l-1+a, q=C0+b. Then q-p = (C0 - (l-1)) + (b-a). So we need convolution of P_shift with a segment of fact starting at K=C0-l+1: G[b]=∑_a P[a] fact[K + b - a]. This is a convolution with a segment of fact of length ~ (C_r-C_{mid+1}) + (mid-l+1). The length can be O(N) even for small node if C range large. But C range over node ≤ C_r-C_l ≤ N. At top O(N), at lower levels sum of C ranges? C is nondecreasing, so sum of C ranges over nodes at a level ≤ N (if ranges partition? C ranges of disjoint j intervals may overlap? Since C nondecreasing, for nodes at same level, their C ranges are ordered and disjoint in terms of j but C values can be same; sum of (C_r-C_l+1) over nodes ≤ N + number nodes? Because C_r of one ≤ C_l of next, ranges may touch, total span ≤N. Good! Similarly p index ranges partition [1,N], total length N. Thus at each CDQ level, if we do NTTs with length proportional to (p_range_length + C_range_length + overlap), the total length per level is O(N + number_nodes). Therefore total O(N log N) NTT work (sum of lengths log lengths per level maybe O(N log^2 N)? Let's analyze: At each node, NTT length L_node ~ nextpow2(p_len + C_range + 1). Sum over nodes at a level of L_node log L_node. Since L_node can be dominated by max of p_len and C_range. The sum of L_node over level ≤ O(N + #nodes * small). But log L_node varies. But maybe with careful global convolution per level (batching nodes) we can get O(N log N). However implementing batching complex.

Let's revisit the CDQ in active c domain and see if we can make it efficient enough in Python with optimizations and special cases. The number of active c m is at most N. In worst-case alternating, m=N-1 and d=0. We can handle d=0 with polynomial inverse O(N log N). In worst-case constant large d, m≈N/2 and d large. Can we handle constant d with a shifted polynomial inverse? If d_t is constant over a contiguous segment, yes: kernel fact[c_t-p_s] = fact[(c_t-c_s)+d]. If d constant for all s in a segment and for pairs within segment? If d_s constant D for all s in the segment, then c_s-p_s=D. For s,t in segment, c_t-p_s = (c_t-c_s)+D. If c_t-c_s is not equal to t-s? c_t may increase by variable amounts (white run lengths). But if we use the actual c values, the kernel depends on c_t-c_s, not t-s. However, we can build arrays indexed by c (not t) for the segment: A at p_s=c_s-D, query at c_t. The difference is c_t - (c_s-D) = (c_t-c_s)+D. This is a standard convolution over the c-coordinate! If the c values in the segment span a range Lc, and p values are c-D, the convolution length is O(c_range), not O(m+D). If c_range is large due to large white jumps, but total c_range over segments? c is white count from 0..N, so total span N. This suggests an algorithm over the c-coordinate rather than t-index.

In active c domain, c_t are distinct white counts (0..N-1). p_t = L_{c_t} (black count before c-th white run). The difference d_t=c_t-p_t. The recurrence for y_t (H at p_t) is:
For active t in increasing c_t:
y_t = -1/fact[d_t] * ( fact[c_t] + ∑_{s<t} y_s fact[c_t - p_s] ).
This is like an online convolution in the c-coordinate: We have updates at position p_s with value y_s, and queries at position c_t: sum updates y_s fact[c_t - p_s]. The update position p_s is ≤ c_t. The query positions c_t are increasing and within 0..N. This is a standard online convolution where updates occur at positions p_s (which are increasing but can be far behind queries) and queries at positions c_t (increasing). The kernel is fact[query - update]. This is exactly the problem of computing a convolution online with arbitrary update positions and query positions, both increasing, over a domain of size N (white count). We can solve this with a CDQ over the c-coordinate (or over the combined sequence of events) in O(N log^2 N) or O(N log N) with relaxed convolution. But the domain size is N=2e5, and the number of events m≤N. This is more standard: We have an array A[p] (updates) that becomes known at time when c_t is processed (but p_t may be < current c). We need to answer queries Q[c_t] = ∑_{p≤c_t} A[p] fact[c_t-p], where A[p] is set at the same time as query c_t (with p=p_t) after computing Q. Note A[p] may be set at a position p that is far less than current c, but we can retroactively add it to the array. Future queries with larger c will include it. This is an online convolution with delayed updates at past positions. But since the domain is just c (0..N), and updates are at positions p_t that are increasing? p_t = L_{c_t} is nondecreasing with c_t (first black count before c-th white run). Yes p_t increases (strictly? If multiple active c in same white run? Only one active per run, p increases by at least1 when a black occurs. So p_t is strictly increasing with t). Query c_t also strictly increasing. Both update positions and query positions are increasing, but update position p_t can be much less than query c_t. However, p_t < c_t (d_t≥0). The update is made at time t, after query c_t, at position p_t which is in the past (≤c_t). For future queries c_u>c_t, the update should be included. This is like we are filling A[p_t] retroactively, but p_t is increasing, so we never update a position that has already been passed by queries? Wait p_t may be less than current query c_t, and there may have been previous queries c_s with c_s > p_t? Since p_t is increasing but can lag behind c_t. Example early many whites: c_t=D+t, p_t=t+1. At t=1, query c=D+1, update p=2. Previous queries? none. Future queries c>D+1, p=2 is in the past relative to the c-domain, but we are adding A[2] after we've already "passed" position 2 in the c-domain. Standard online convolution assumes updates at index i become available before queries with index >i, but here update at p=2 is added after query c=D+1 (which is >2). For future queries, it's fine, but for the convolution at c=D+1, it should not be included (correct, because H at p=2 is determined by this query). For any query c between 2 and D+1 (if there were active c values), the update should not be included, but there may be none (c values skipped). If there were active c values in between, p_t would have been set earlier? Since p_t increasing, if c values skipped, no queries. So retroactive update at p_t after passing p_t is okay as long as there are no future queries with c < p_t (none, c increases). But for convolution algorithms that process c from 0 to N sequentially, adding A[p] after we've passed p is nonstandard but can be handled because it only affects future c>p. We can treat it as a point update at position p with value A[p] at the current time, and future point queries at c. This is a dynamic data structure for convolution: support point update A[p]+=val, point query Q[c]=∑_{p≤c} A[p] fact[c-p], with updates and queries interleaved in increasing c, but updates can be at p≤current c. This is exactly a "online convolution with delayed updates". Can we support this in O(log^2 N) per operation using a Fenwick tree of NTT? Maybe.

Since the kernel is fact[c-p], we can use a divide-and-conquer over the c-domain offline: We know all update positions p_t and query positions c_t in advance, and the value A[p_t] depends on previous query results. This is a triangular system over events ordered by c_t. CDQ over events (t) is natural. But maybe because the domain c is 1D and updates/queries are monotone, we can use a segment tree over c with lazy polynomial evaluations. Each update at p adds a function f_p(c)=fact[c-p] for c≥p. We need point queries at c_t. This is a range update (from p to N) of a function that is a shifted factorial, and point query. Can we decompose fact[c-p] into a basis of functions that allow range update/point query with O(log N) and small dimension? As noted, fact[c-p] as a function of c for fixed p is not a polynomial of bounded degree. But over finite field and for c up to N, the set of functions {fact[c-p]} for p=0..N is a basis of dimension N. Range update/point query in this basis is just maintaining A[p]; point query is convolution. No low-dimensional basis.

However, we can use a Fenwick tree where each node stores a polynomial (NTT) of updates in its range, and query accumulates O(log N) convolutions? Point query would need to combine O(log N) polynomials evaluated at c-p? Not easy.

Maybe use the fact that updates and queries are monotone to apply a "sqrt decomposition" over c-domain. Divide the c-domain into blocks of size B. For updates in a block, we can precompute their contributions to future query blocks using NTT. Since updates are added over time (but all updates in a block occur when c reaches certain values, which may be after the block's c range). We can process c blocks sequentially. For each block of query c values [L,R], contributions from updates with p < L can be computed by a convolution of all A[p] for p<L (known) with fact, sampled at c in [L,R]. Contributions from updates with p in [L,R] (same block) can be computed directly O(B^2) because p and c in same block. Updates with p > R cannot affect c in [L,R] (since need p≤c). This is a standard block decomposition for online convolution! Complexity: For each query block, we need to convolve the prefix A[0..L-1] with fact to get values at c in [L,R]. If we do an NTT of length O(N) per block, too much. But we can update the convolution incrementally: As we move from block to block, the prefix A gains a block of updates. We can maintain the convolution C[c]=∑_{p<L} A[p] fact[c-p] for all future c? Adding a block of updates (size B) requires convolving that block with fact and adding to C for c≥L. We can do one NTT per update block of length O(N) (or O(N-L)?) => (N/B) NTTs. Choose B ~ sqrt(N) => ~450 NTTs of length 2e5, too much in Python. But we can choose B large, e.g., B=4096 => ~49 NTTs, maybe feasible? Direct within block O(NB) = 2e5*4096=8e8 too high. Need balance. If B=700, NTTs 286, direct 1.4e8 maybe too high. In Python no.

But we can use NTT per block with length proportional to the remaining domain? Still large. Maybe use a two-level sqrt: not.

What if we use the CDQ over c-domain but batch nodes at each level with one NTT? For the standard online convolution with updates at p and queries at c where both are increasing and update p≤query c, there is an O(N log N) algorithm using divide-and-conquer over the domain: At each level, for each node [L,R], updates in left half [L,mid] contribute to queries in right half [mid+1,R]. This is exactly CDQ. The total cost can be O(N log N) if at each level we perform one convolution for the whole level by concatenating all left-update blocks and right-query blocks with appropriate offsets? Let's think.

At a level, nodes partition [0,N]. For each node, left updates indices [L,mid], right queries [mid+1,R]. The contribution is ∑_{p in left} A[p] fact[c-p]. If we create a global array P_level where for each node we place A[p] at position p-L_node? and query c at c-L_node? The offsets differ per node, so a single convolution with a global fact won't align. But we can shift each node's arrays to a common origin by using p and c absolute indices: If we place P[p]=A[p] for all left updates across all nodes, and convolve with fact, the coefficient at c includes updates from all left halves, including those in other nodes. We need to exclude updates from other nodes that are not in the same node's left half. However, for a query c in node [L,R], any update p < L (from earlier nodes) should have been handled at a higher level, not this level. Any update p in [L,mid] should be handled now. Updates p in [mid+1,c] (same node right half) should be handled at lower levels. Updates p in other nodes after L? If p > R, cannot affect c. If p in a previous node [L',R'] with R'<L, p<L, should be excluded (handled higher). If p in a later node, p>R≥c, excluded. Thus the only unwanted contributions in the global convolution are updates p < L from previous nodes at the same level. If we could subtract them, maybe using prefix convolutions. At each level, we need for each node the convolution of A in [L,mid] with fact at c. This is like a range convolution query: for many intervals [L,mid] and points c in [mid+1,R]. There are data structures for offline range convolution using divide-and-conquer, but not trivial.

Let's compute the IE H values for sample3: H0=1,H1=-2,H2=-2,H3=0,H4=0,H5=-60,H6=-372,H7=0,H8=-27696,H9=0. Final answer = ∑ H[j] fact[9-j] = 240792. The H values look like they could be computed by a simple recurrence involving K_j. Let's list j, C_j, K_j=C-j, S_j=Q_j, H_j=-S/fact[K] if K≥0 else0:
j1 C2 K1=1 S=2 H=-2
j2 C3 K=1 S=2 H=-2
j3 C3 K=0 S=0 H=0
j4 C3 K=-1 S=0 H=0
j5 C5 K=0 S=60 H=-60
j6 C6 K=0 S=372 H=-372
j7 C6 K=-1 S=0 H=0
j8 C8 K=0 S=27696 H=-27696
j9 C8 K=-1 S=0 H=0
Notice S_j is zero whenever K_j=-1. When K_j=0, S_j can be nonzero and H=-S. When K_j=1, S_j=2. The S_j maybe follows a recurrence with K. Let's compute S_j directly:
S_j = ∑_{p<j} H[p] fact[C_j-p].
For j with K=0, C=j. S_j = ∑ H[p] fact[j-p]. This is the standard convolution of H with fact. For j=5, H0..4: 1,-2,-2,0,0 => f5 120 -2 f4 24 -2 f3 6 =120-48-12=60. j6: 720 -2*120 -2*24 -60*1? H5=-60 fact1 =>720-240-48-60=372. j8: 40320 -2*5040 -2*720 -60*120 -372*2 =27696. This is like the convolution sequence P_j = ∑_{p=0}^j H[p] fact[j-p]. For K=0, S_j=P_j (excluding H[j] fact0? Actually p<j, H[j] not included; P_j includes H[j]. Since H[j]=-P_j (K=0), P_j=S_j). The recurrence P_j for K=0 is P_j = fact[j] + ∑_{p<j} H[p] fact[j-p] = fact[j] + S_j. But H[j]=-S_j, so P_{j+1}? This is the inverse factorial series. For K=1, S_j = ∑ H[p] fact[j+1-p] = P_{j+1}? Since C=j+1. S_j = P_{j+1} (with p<j, missing H[j]? P_{j+1}=∑_{p≤j+1} H[p] fact[j+1-p], includes H[j]fact1 and H[j+1]fact0. Not same.) For j1 K1=1, S1=fact2=2. P2=H0 f2+H1 f1+H2 f0 =2-2+H2? H2 unknown. Not.

Maybe we can maintain the full convolution P_j = ∑_{p=0}^j H[p] fact[j-p] for all j (the product H*fact). The IE condition says for active j with K_j≥0, H[j] = -S_j/fact[K_j], where S_j = ∑_{p<j} H[p] fact[K_j+j-p] = P_{K_j+j}? Not exactly P_{C_j} with H[p] for p<C_j? P_{C_j}=∑_{p=0}^{C_j} H[p] fact[C_j-p]. But H[p] for p>j? Since C_j may be >j, P_{C_j} includes H[p] for j<p≤C_j, which are not determined yet (and may be zero if p not active? But p is black index, H[p] defined for all p, not just active c. If p>j, H[p] future. Our S_j excludes p≥j. So S_j is a partial convolution up to p<j. If C_j>j, the full P_{C_j} would include future H[p] (p between j+1 and C_j). Those future H[p] may be nonzero? p is black index, C_j can be much larger than j (many whites before next black). But H[p] for p>j with p≤C_j: Are they determined later, but they should not affect S_j. In the zeroing process over active c, the update p_t=L_c can be much less than c, and the query c includes only updates with p_t < current c_t, not all p≤c. In the black-index H array, future H[p] for p>j but p≤C_j are not included. So P_{C_j} is not the right object.

However, in the active c domain, updates are at p_t (black index) and queries at c_t (white count). The query c_t may be much larger than the current black index t, but updates with p between current t+1 and c_t correspond to future black indices that have not been processed, even though p≤c. They are not included. So the online convolution is over events ordered by t (black index), not by p or c. The update position p_t is roughly t (or less), and query position c_t is roughly t+d.
